**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Substitute \(x = 3\) into the expression:**
   \[
   2(3)^2 - 5(3) + 1
   \]

2. **Calculate each term separately:**
   - Calculate \(3^2\):
     \[
     3^2 = 9
     \]
   - Multiply by 2:
     \[
     2 \times 9 = 18
     \]
   - Multiply 5 by 3:
     \[
     5 \times 3 = 15
     \]

3. **Combine all the terms:**
   \[
   18 - 15 + 1 = 4
   \]

To evaluate the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 1}} \frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Indeterminate Form:**
   When \( x = 1 \), both the numerator and denominator become zero, resulting in the indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \).

2. **Factor the Numerator:**
   Use the difference of cubes formula to factor \( x^3 - 1 \):
   \[
   x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)
   \]

3. **Simplify the Expression:**
   Substitute the factored form into the original expression and cancel the common term \( (x - 1) \):
   \[
   \frac{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1} = x^2 + x + 1 \quad \text{for } x \neq 1
   \]

4. **Evaluate the Simplified Limit:**
   Now, substitute \( x = 1 \) into the simplified expression:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 1}} (x^2 + x + 1) = 1^2 + 1 + 1 = 3
   \]

   \[

   \]

   \[
   \lim_{\theta \to 0} \frac{\sin(\theta)}{\theta/2} = \lim_{\theta \to 0} \frac{2\sin(\theta)}{\theta}

The limit of ln(1+x)/x as x approaches 0 can be evaluated using two methods: Taylor series expansion or L'Hospital's Rule.

1. **Taylor Series Expansion**:
   - Expand ln(1+x) around x=0: ln(1+x) ≈ x - x²/2 + x³/3 - ...
   - Divide by x: (x - x²/2 + ...)/x = 1 - x/2 + x²/3 - ...
   - As x approaches 0, higher power terms vanish, leaving the limit as 1.

2. **L'Hospital's Rule**:
   - Since both numerator and denominator approach 0, apply L’Hospital’s Rule.
   - Derivative of numerator: d/dx [ln(1+x)] = 1/(1+x)
   - Derivative of denominator: d/dx [x] = 1
   - New limit becomes lim_{x→0} (1/(1+x)) / 1 = 1.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

   As \( x \to 0 \), both numerator and denominator approach 0:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 0}} (x - \sin(x)) = 0 \\
   \lim_{{x \to 0}} x^3 = 0
   \]

   Differentiate numerator and denominator:
   \[
   \text{Numerator derivative}: \frac{d}{dx}(x - \sin(x)) = 1 - \cos(x) \\

   \]

   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{1 - \cos(x)}{3x^2}
   \]

   \[
   \text{Numerator derivative}: \frac{d}{dx}(1 - \cos(x)) = \sin(x) \\
   \text{Denominator derivative}: \frac{d}{dx} 3x^2 = 6x
   \]
   The limit now is:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sin(x)}{6x}
   \]

4. **Simplify the Expression:**
   Use the known limit \( \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sin(x)}{x} = 1 \):
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sin(x)}{6x} = \frac{1}{6}

The limit as \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side for \( x^x \) can be evaluated using logarithmic transformation and L'Hospital's Rule.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Let \( y = x^x \).**
2. **Take natural logarithm on both sides:**  
   \( \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x) \).
3. **Evaluate the limit of \( \ln(y) \):**  
   \( \lim_{x\to0^+} \ln(y) = \lim_{x\to0^+} x \cdot \ln(x) \).
4. **Recognize indeterminate form:**  
   As \( x \to 0^+ \), \( \ln(x) \to -\infty \) and \( x \to 0 \), leading to \( 0 \cdot (-\infty) \). Rewrite as:
   \( \lim_{x\to0^+} \frac{\ln(x)}{1/x} \).
5. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**  
   Differentiate numerator and denominator:  
   \( \frac{d}{dx} \ln(x) = 1/x \),  
   \( \frac{d}{dx} (1/x) = -1/x^2 \).  
   So, the limit becomes:
   \( \lim_{x\to0^+} \frac{1/x}{-1/x^2} = \lim_{x\to0^+} (-x) = 0 \).
6. **Exponentiate to find original limit:**  
   Since \( \ln(y) \to 0 \), then \( y = e^{0} = 1 \).

**Final Answer:**
The limit is \( 1 \).

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of \( \frac{\ln x}{x} \), we analyze the behavior of both the numerator and the denominator.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Behavior of Numerator and Denominator:**
   - As \( x \to \infty \), \( \ln x \) increases without bound, but at a slower rate compared to linear functions.
   - The denominator \( x \) grows without bound at a linear rate.

   - Both the numerator and the denominator approach infinity as \( x \to \infty \), resulting in an indeterminate form of \( \frac{\infty}{\infty} \).
   - Differentiate both the numerator and the denominator with respect to \( x \):
     \[
     \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{\ln x}{x} = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{\frac{d}{dx}(\ln x)}{\frac{d}{dx}(x)} = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{\frac{1}{x}}{1}
     \]

3. **Evaluate the Simplified Limit:**
   - As \( x \) approaches infinity, \( \frac{1}{x} \) approaches 0.

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{0}
\]

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches 1 of the function \( \frac{(1 + x^3)^{1/3} - 1}{x - 1} \), we can use L'Hospital's Rule because both the numerator and the denominator approach 0.

First, we check if applying L'Hospital's Rule is valid. Both the numerator and the denominator are differentiable functions that approach 0 as \( x \) approaches 1.

Applying L'Hospital's Rule involves taking the derivatives of the numerator and the denominator:

1. The derivative of the numerator \( (1 + x^3)^{1/3} - 1 \):
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}\left( (1 + x^3)^{1/3} - 1 \right) = \frac{1}{3}(1 + x^3)^{-2/3} \cdot 3x^2 = x^2 (1 + x^3)^{-2/3}
   \]

2. The derivative of the denominator \( x - 1 \):
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(x - 1) = 1
   \]

Thus, applying L'Hospital's Rule, we get:
\[
\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{x^2 (1 + x^3)^{-2/3}}{1}
\]

Evaluating this at \( x = 1 \):
\[
(1)^2 (1 + (1)^3)^{-2/3} = 1^{-2/3} = 1
\]
But wait, we need to correctly evaluate the expression:
\[
(1)^2 (1 + 1^3)^{-2/3} = 1 \cdot (2)^{-2/3}
\]

Therefore, the limit simplifies to:
\[
2^{-2/3} = \frac{1}{2^{2/3}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{4}}
\]

Thus, the final answer is:
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{1}{\sqrt[3]{4}}}
\]

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we will apply the basic rules of differentiation.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Differentiate each term separately using the power rule:**
   - The derivative of \( x^n \) is \( nx^{n-1} \).
   
2. **Apply the power rule to each term:**
   - For \( 3x^2 \):
     - Coefficient = 3
     - Exponent = 2
     - Derivative = \( 3 \times 2x^{2-1} = 6x \)
     
   - For \( -5x \) (which can be written as \( -5x^1 \)):
     - Coefficient = -5
     - Exponent = 1
     - Derivative = \( -5 \times 1x^{1-1} = -5 \)

   - For the constant term \( +4 \):
     - The derivative of a constant is 0.

3. **Combine the derivatives:**
   - \( f'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 \)
   - Simplifying, we get \( f'(x) = 6x - 5 \)

**Final Answer:**
\[
f'(x) = \boxed{6x - 5}
\]

   - Using the power rule: 
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}\left( x^{-1} \right) = -1 \cdot x^{-2}
     \]

To find the derivative of \( x^x \), we can use logarithmic differentiation. Here's a step-by-step explanation:

**Step 1: Let \( y = x^x \)**

We start by letting:
\[ y = x^x \]

**Step 2: Take the natural logarithm of both sides**

Applying the natural logarithm to both sides helps simplify the differentiation process:
\[ \ln y = \ln(x^x) \]
Using the property \( \ln(a^b) = b\ln a \):
\[ \ln y = x \ln x \]

**Step 3: Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \)**

Differentiate implicitly with respect to \( x \):
\[ \frac{d}{dx} (\ln y) = \frac{d}{dx} (x \ln x) \]
Using the chain rule on the left side and applying the product rule on the right side:
\[ \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1 \cdot \ln x + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} \]

**Step 4: Simplify the equation**

Simplify both sides of the equation:
\[ \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \ln x + 1 \]

**Step 5: Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \)**

Multiply both sides by \( y \) to solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):
\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = y (\ln x + 1) \]

**Step 6: Substitute back \( y = x^x \)**

Finally, substitute \( y = x^x \) back into the equation:
\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = x^x (\ln x + 1) \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\frac{d}{dx}(x^x) = x^x (1 + \ln x)}
\]

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = x \cdot e^x \), we'll use the **product rule**. The product rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), then its derivative is:

\[
h'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

Let's apply this to our function.

1. **Identify the functions:**
   - Let \( u(x) = x \)
   - Let \( v(x) = e^x \)

2. **Find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):**
   - \( u'(x) = 1 \) (since the derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is 1)
   - \( v'(x) = e^x \) (since the derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is itself)

3. **Apply the product rule:**
   \[
   f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
   \]
   Substituting the derivatives we found:
   \[
   f'(x) = 1 \cdot e^x + x \cdot e^x
   \]

4. **Simplify the expression:**
   Factor out \( e^x \):
   \[
   f'(x) = (1 + x)e^x
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{(1 + x)e^{x}}

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \), we'll follow these steps:

1. **Rewrite the Function**:  
   It's often easier to differentiate functions in their current form, but rewriting it as:
   \[
   f(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1}
   \]
   allows us to use the chain rule more effectively.

2. **Apply the Chain Rule**:  
   The chain rule states that if \( h(x) = g(f(x)) \), then \( h'(x) = g'(f(x)) \cdot f'(x) \). In this case, let’s identify:
   - Outer function: \( g(u) = u^{-1} \)
   - Inner function: \( u = x^2 + 1 \)

3. **Differentiate the Outer Function**:  
   The derivative of \( g(u) = u^{-1} \) with respect to \( u \) is:
   \[
   g'(u) = -u^{-2}
   \]

4. **Differentiate the Inner Function**:  
   The derivative of \( u = x^2 + 1 \) with respect to \( x \) is:
   \[
   u' = 2x
   \]

5. **Combine Using the Chain Rule**:  
   Multiply the derivatives obtained from steps 3 and 4:
   \[
   f'(x) = g'(u) \cdot u' = -u^{-2} \cdot 2x = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
   \]

6. **Final Answer**:  
   Therefore, the derivative of \( f(x) \) is:
   \[
   \boxed{-\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}}
   \]

The derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{\ln x}{x^2} \) is calculated using the quotient rule.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify Functions for Quotient Rule:**
   - Let \( g(x) = \ln x \)
   - Let \( h(x) = x^2 \)

2. **Find Derivatives of \( g(x) \) and \( h(x) \):**
   - \( g'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \)
   - \( h'(x) = 2x \)

3. **Apply the Quotient Rule:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2}
   \]
   
4. **Substitute \( g(x) \), \( h(x) \), \( g'(x) \), and \( h'(x) \):**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)(x^2) - (\ln x)(2x)}{(x^2)^2}
   \]

5. **Simplify the Numerator:**
   - First term: \( \frac{x^2}{x} = x \)
   - Second term: \( 2x \ln x \)
   - So, numerator becomes: \( x - 2x \ln x \)

6. **Express the Simplified Numerator as a Single Term:**
   - Factor out \( x \): \( x(1 - 2 \ln x) \)

7. **Write the Entire Expression with the Denominator:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{x(1 - 2 \ln x)}{x^4}
   \]

8. **Simplify the Fraction by Canceling Common Terms:**
   - \( \frac{x}{x^4} = \frac{1}{x^3} \)
   - So, final expression becomes:
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{1 - 2 \ln x}{x^3}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of the function is \( \frac{1 - 2 \ln x}{x^3} \).

Let me start by recalling some basic rules. For derivatives, if I have a function that's a product of two functions, say u(x) and v(x), then the first derivative is found using the product rule: (u*v)' = u'*v + u*v'.

3x² ln x + x²

So, derivative is u'(x)*v(x) + u(x)*v'(x).

6x * ln x

The second part of this term's derivative is 3x² * (1/x) = 3x.

Now, moving on to the second term: d/dx [x²] = 2x.

F''(x) = (6x ln x + 3x) + 2x

Simplify by combining like terms. The 3x and 2x can be added:

6x ln x + 5x

Alternatively, factor out the x if needed: x*(6 ln x + 5)

The derivative of the function \( f(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \) is found using the quotient rule. The steps are as follows:

1. **Identify the numerator and denominator:**
   - Numerator (\( g(x) \)) = \( e^x \)
   - Denominator (\( h(x) \)) = \( 1 + e^x \)

2. **Compute the derivatives of the numerator and denominator:**
   - \( g'(x) = e^x \)
   - \( h'(x) = e^x \)

3. **Apply the quotient rule formula:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2} = \frac{e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x)}{(1 + e^x)^2}
   \]

4. **Simplify the expression:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{e^x + e^{2x} - e^{2x}}{(1 + e^x)^2} = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{\dfrac{e^x}{\left(1+e^x\right)^2}}

To find the derivative of \( \tan^{-1} x \) with respect to \( x \), we can use implicit differentiation.

Let:
\[ y = \tan^{-1} x \]
This implies:
\[ \tan y = x \]

Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):
\[ \frac{d}{dx} (\tan y) = \frac{d}{dx} (x) \]
Using the chain rule on the left side:
\[ \sec^2 y \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1 \]

Solving for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):
\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2 y} \]

Since \( \tan y = x \), we can use the identity:
\[ \sec^2 y = 1 + \tan^2 y = 1 + x^2 \]

Substituting back, we get:
\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{1 + x^2} \]

**Final Answer:**
The derivative of \( \tan^{-1} x \) with respect to \( x \) is \(\boxed{\dfrac{1}{1 + x^{2}}}\).

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = x \ln(x) \), we will use the **product rule**. The product rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), then the derivative \( h'(x) \) is given by:

\[
h'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

**Step 1: Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**
- Let \( u(x) = x \)
- Let \( v(x) = \ln(x) \)

**Step 2: Find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**
- The derivative of \( u(x) = x \) with respect to \( x \) is:
\[
u'(x) = 1
\]
- The derivative of \( v(x) = \ln(x) \) with respect to \( x \) is:
\[
v'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
\]

**Step 3: Apply the product rule**
Substitute these derivatives back into the product rule formula:
\[
f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]
\[
f'(x) = 1 \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x}
\]

**Step 4: Simplify the expression**
Simplify each term:
- \( 1 \cdot \ln(x) = \ln(x) \)
- \( x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = 1 \)

So, combining these results:
\[
f'(x) = \ln(x) + 1
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\ln(x) + 1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **First Derivative:**
   - Given F(x) = e^{x²}, apply the chain rule.
   - Let u = x²; then du/dx = 2x.
   - dF/dx = e^{u} * du/dx = e^{x²} * 2x.

2. **Second Derivative:**
   - Now, differentiate the first derivative, which is f'(x) = 2x * e^{x²}.
   - Use the product rule: if f(x) = u*v, then f''(x) = u'v + uv'.
     - Let u = 2x ⇒ u' = 2.
     - Let v = e^{x²} ⇒ v' = 2x * e^{x²}.
   - Apply the product rule:
     - f''(x) = (u')*v + u*(v') = 2*e^{x²} + (2x)*(2x e^{x²}).
     - Simplify: f''(x) = 2e^{x²} + 4x² e^{x²}.
     - Factor out common terms: f''(x) = (2 + 4x²)e^{x²}.

The function \( f(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \) can be differentiated using the quotient rule. The derivative is calculated as follows:

- Let \( g(x) = x \) and \( h(x) = 1 + x^2 \).
- Compute their derivatives: \( g'(x) = 1 \) and \( h'(x) = 2x \).
- Apply the quotient rule:
  
  \[
  f'(x) = \frac{g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2} = \frac{(1)(1 + x^2) - (x)(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}
  \]
  
- Simplify the numerator:
  
  \[
  1 + x^2 - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2
  \]

The derivative of \(\sec x\) with respect to \(x\) is found using the reciprocal rule for derivatives.

Let \(u = \cos x\), so \(\sec x = \frac{1}{u}\).

Using the derivative rule:
\[
\frac{d}{dx} \left( \frac{1}{u} \right) = -\frac{u'}{u^2}
\]

The derivative of \(u = \cos x\) is:
\[
u' = -\sin x
\]

Substituting back into the formula:
\[
\frac{d}{dx} (\sec x) = -\frac{-\sin x}{(\cos x)^2} = \frac{\sin x}{\cos^2 x}
\]

This simplifies to:
\[
\sec x \tan x
\]

L'Hospital's Rule says that if we have an indeterminate form of type 0/0 or ∞/∞, then the limit is equal to the limit of the derivatives of the numerator and denominator.

The squeeze theorem states that if we have three functions f(x), g(x), and h(x) such that f(x) ≤ g(x) ≤ h(x) near x = a (except possibly at a itself), and lim_{x→a} f(x) = lim_{x→a} h(x) = L, then lim_{x→a} g(x) is also L.

For sin(x)/x as x approaches 0 from the right, we know that cos(x) ≤ (sin x)/x ≤ 1. As x approaches 0, cos(x) approaches 1, so by the squeeze theorem, (sin x)/x must also approach 1.

The limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{\sin(x)}{x} \) can be evaluated using multiple methods:

1. **L'Hospital's Rule**: Since substituting \( x = 0 \) results in an indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \), we apply L'Hospital's Rule by differentiating the numerator and denominator:
   - Derivative of \( \sin(x) \) is \( \cos(x) \).
   - Derivative of \( x \) is 1.
   Thus, the limit becomes \( \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\cos(x)}{1} = 1 \).

2. **Taylor Series Expansion**: The Taylor series expansion of \( \sin(x) \) around 0 is:
   \[
   \sin(x) = x - \frac{x^3}{6} + \cdots
   \]
   Dividing by \( x \), we get:
   \[
   \frac{\sin(x)}{x} = 1 - \frac{x^2}{6} + \cdots
   \]
   As \( x \to 0 \), the higher-order terms vanish, leaving a limit of 1.

3. **Squeeze Theorem**: For \( x > 0 \) near 0, it's known that:
   \[
   \cos(x) \leq \frac{\sin(x)}{x} \leq 1
   \]
   As \( x \to 0 \), both bounds approach 1, so by the squeeze theorem, the limit is 1.

All methods confirm that the limit is 1.

To find the limit:

\[
\lim_{{x \to 1}} \frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Indeterminate Form:**
   When \( x = 1 \),
   \[
   \frac{1^3 - 1}{1 - 1} = \frac{0}{0}
   \]
   This is an indeterminate form, so we need to simplify the expression.

2. **Factor the Numerator:**
   The numerator \( x^3 - 1 \) is a difference of cubes, which can be factored using the formula:
   \[
   a^3 - b^3 = (a - b)(a^2 + ab + b^2)
   \]
   Here, \( a = x \) and \( b = 1 \), so,
   \[
   x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)
   \]

3. **Simplify the Expression:**
   Substitute the factored form of the numerator into the original limit:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 1}} \frac{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1}
   \]
   Cancel out the \( (x - 1) \) terms in the numerator and denominator:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 1}} (x^2 + x + 1)
   \]

4. **Evaluate the Simplified Limit:**
   Now, substitute \( x = 1 \) into the simplified expression:
   \[
   1^2 + 1 + 1 = 3
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{3}

Given this, L'Hospital's Rule can be applied since it helps evaluate limits involving indeterminate forms like \( \frac{0}{0} \) or \( \frac{\infty}{\infty} \). 

Applying L'Hospital's Rule involves taking the derivatives of both the numerator and the denominator. The derivative of \( \tan x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \sec^2 x \), and the derivative of \( x \) is 1.

So, after differentiation, the expression becomes:
\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sec^2 x}{1}
\]

Evaluating this simplified expression at \( x = 0 \), we know that \( \cos(0) = 1 \), so \( \sec(0) = \frac{1}{\cos(0)} = 1 \). Therefore, \( \sec^2 (0) = 1^2 = 1 \).

\]

To evaluate the limit:

\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\tan x}{x}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Problem:**
   We need to find the value of \( \frac{\tan x}{x} \) as \( x \) approaches 0.

2. **Evaluate Direct Substitution:**
   - When \( x = 0 \):
     - \( \tan(0) = 0 \)
     - So, \( \frac{0}{0} \), which is an indeterminate form.
   
3. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   Since we have an indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \), we can use L'Hospital's Rule:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\tan x}{x} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\frac{d}{dx}(\tan x)}{\frac{d}{dx}(x)}
   \]
   
4. **Differentiate the Numerator and Denominator:**
   - \( \frac{d}{dx}(\tan x) = \sec^2 x \)
   - \( \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1 \)

5. **Simplify the Expression:**
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sec^2 x}{1} = \lim_{x \to 0} \sec^2 x
   \]
   
6. **Evaluate at \( x = 0 \):**
   - \( \cos(0) = 1 \)
   - So, \( \sec(0) = \frac{1}{\cos(0)} = 1 \)
   - Therefore, \( \sec^2(0) = 1^2 = 1 \)

7. **Final Answer:**
   \[
   \boxed{1}
   \]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Recognize the Form:** The given expression, \( (1 + \frac{1}{x})^x \), is a standard limit form encountered in calculus.
   
2. **Recall the Definition of e:** The number \( e \) is defined as:
   \[
   e = \lim_{n \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{1}{n}\right)^n
   \]
   This definition directly matches our given expression.

3. **Substitute Variables if Needed:** If you prefer, let \( n = x \). As \( x \) approaches infinity, so does \( n \), ensuring the substitution is valid.

4. **Conclusion:** Since the form of the limit exactly matches the definition of \( e \), we can conclude that:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right)^x = e
   \]

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of the function \( \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1} \), I will follow these steps:

First, observe that both the numerator and the denominator are quadratic polynomials. When evaluating limits at infinity for rational functions where the degrees of the numerator and denominator are equal, the limit is determined by the ratio of their leading coefficients.

The highest degree term in both the numerator and the denominator is \( x^2 \). The coefficient of \( x^2 \) in the numerator is 1, and in the denominator it is also 1. 

Therefore, dividing these leading coefficients gives:

\[
\frac{1}{1} = 1
\]

Hence, the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of the given function is 1.
</think>

To evaluate the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1}
\]
follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the Degrees of the Numerator and Denominator
Both the numerator \( x^2 + x + 1 \) and the denominator \( x^2 - x + 1 \) are quadratic polynomials (degree 2).

### Step 2: Determine the Leading Coefficients
The leading term in both polynomials is \( x^2 \).
- Leading coefficient of the numerator: **1**
- Leading coefficient of the denominator: **1**

### Step 3: Evaluate the Limit
When evaluating limits at infinity for rational functions where the degrees of the numerator and denominator are equal, the limit is simply the ratio of their leading coefficients.

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1} = \frac{1}{1} = 1
\]

The given expression is:
\[ \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(1 + x^3)^{1/3} - 1}{x - 1} \]

As \( x \) approaches 1, both numerator and denominator approach zero since the numerator becomes approximately 0.26 (not exactly zero) and denominator is zero. Therefore, we can apply L'Hospital's Rule:

Differentiate numerator:
\[ f(x) = (1 + x^3)^{1/3} \]
\[ f'(x) = \frac{1}{3}(1 + x^3)^{-2/3} \cdot 3x^2 = \frac{x^2}{(1 + x^3)^{2/3}} \]

Differentiate denominator:
\[ g(x) = x - 1 \]
\[ g'(x) = 1 \]

Applying L'Hospital's Rule:
\[ \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)} = \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{x^2}{(1 + x^3)^{2/3}} = \frac{1^2}{(1 + 1^3)^{2/3}} = \frac{1}{2^{2/3}} \]

Thus, the limit is \( \frac{1}{2^{2/3}} \).

Answer: {Final Answer: "Numeric answer": {"value": 0.63}}

To find the derivative of \( \frac{d}{dx}(x^x) \), we can use logarithmic differentiation.

1. Let \( y = x^x \).
2. Take the natural logarithm of both sides: \( \ln(y) = \ln(x^x) \).
3. Simplify using logarithm properties: \( \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x) \).
4. Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):
   - Left side: \( \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} \).
   - Right side (using product rule on \( x \cdot \ln(x) \)): \( 1 \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \ln(x) + 1 \).
5. Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \): \( \frac{dy}{dx} = y (\ln(x) + 1) \).
6. Substitute \( y = x^x \): \( \frac{dy}{dx} = x^x (\ln(x) + 1) \).

**Final Answer:**  
The derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( x^x (\ln(x) + 1) \).

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = x \cdot e^x \), we can use the **product rule** from calculus. The product rule states that if you have a function that is the product of two other functions, say \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \), then the derivative of the product is given by:

\[
f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the two functions in the product:**
   - Let \( u(x) = x \)
   - Let \( v(x) = e^x \)

2. **Find the derivatives of each function:**
   - The derivative of \( u(x) = x \) is:
     \[
     u'(x) = 1
     \]
   - The derivative of \( v(x) = e^x \) remains:
     \[
     v'(x) = e^x
     \]

3. **Apply the product rule:**
   \[
   f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
   \]
   Substituting the derivatives we found:
   \[
   f'(x) = 1 \cdot e^x + x \cdot e^x
   \]

4. **Simplify the expression:**
   Factor out \( e^x \):
   \[
   f'(x) = e^x (1 + x)
   \]
   
**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{f'(x) = e^{x}(x + 1)}
\]

To find the derivative of \( \frac{d}{dx} (e^{x^2}) \), we can use the chain rule.

**Chain Rule Steps:**

1. **Identify the outer and inner functions:**
   - Outer function (\( f(u) \)): \( e^u \)
   - Inner function (\( u(g(x)) \)): \( g(x) = x^2 \)

2. **Differentiate the outer function with respect to the inner function:**
   \[
   \frac{d}{du} (e^u) = e^u
   \]

3. **Differentiate the inner function with respect to \( x \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (x^2) = 2x
   \]

4. **Multiply the derivatives together:**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (e^{x^2}) = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x = 2x e^{x^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{2x e^{x^2}}

To find the derivative of \( \tan^{-1} x \), we'll use differentiation techniques involving inverse trigonometric functions and implicit differentiation. Here's a step-by-step solution:

1. **Let** \( y = \tan^{-1} x \).

2. **Express in terms of tangent:**
   \[
   \tan y = x
   \]

3. **Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (\tan y) = \frac{d}{dx} (x)
   \]
   
4. **Apply the chain rule on the left side:**
   \[
   \sec^2 y \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1
   \]
   
5. **Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):**
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2 y}
   \]
   
6. **Express \( \sec^2 y \) in terms of \( x \):**
   Since \( \tan y = x \), we know that:
   \[
   \sec^2 y = 1 + \tan^2 y = 1 + x^2
   \]
   
7. **Substitute back:**
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\frac{1}{1+x^{2}}}

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = x \ln x \), I will use the product rule. 

The product rule states that if you have a function that is the product of two functions, say u(x) and v(x), then the derivative is:

\[ (u \cdot v)' = u' \cdot v + u \cdot v' \]

In this case, let \( u(x) = x \) and \( v(x) = \ln x \).

First, I'll find the derivatives of u(x) and v(x):

- The derivative of \( u(x) = x \) with respect to x is \( u'(x) = 1 \).
- The derivative of \( v(x) = \ln x \) with respect to x is \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \).

Now, applying the product rule:

\[ f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x) \]
\[ f'(x) = 1 \cdot \ln x + x \cdot \left( \frac{1}{x} \right) \]

Simplifying the expression:

\[ f'(x) = \ln x + 1 \]

So, the derivative of \( f(x) = x \ln x \) is \( \ln x + 1 \).

\[
(u \cdot v)' = u' \cdot v + u \cdot v'
\]

**Step 1: Identify the functions**

Let:
- \( u(x) = x \)
- \( v(x) = \ln x \)

**Step 2: Find the derivatives of each function**

- The derivative of \( u(x) = x \) is:
  \[
  u'(x) = 1
  \]
  
- The derivative of \( v(x) = \ln x \) is:
  \[
  v'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
  \]

\[
f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]
\[
f'(x) = 1 \cdot \ln x + x \cdot \left( \frac{1}{x} \right)

**Step 4: Simplify the expression**

\[

The second derivative of \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \) is found using the product rule and chain rule, resulting in \( F''(x) = (4x^2 + 2)e^{x^2} \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **First Derivative (F'(x))**:
   - Apply the chain rule to \( e^{x^2} \).
   - Let \( u = x^2 \), so \( F(x) = e^u \).
   - The derivative of \( e^u \) with respect to \( u \) is \( e^u \).
   - Multiply by the derivative of \( u \) with respect to \( x \): \( 2x \).
   - Therefore, \( F'(x) = 2x e^{x^2} \).

2. **Second Derivative (F''(x))**:
   - Apply the product rule to \( F'(x) = 2x e^{x^2} \).
   - Let \( f(x) = 2x \) and \( g(x) = e^{x^2} \).
   - Compute derivatives:
     - \( f'(x) = 2 \)
     - \( g'(x) = 2x e^{x^2} \) (using the chain rule again for \( e^{x^2} \)).
   - Apply the product rule: \( F''(x) = f'(x)g(x) + f(x)g'(x) \).
     - Substitute values:
       \( 2 * e^{x^2} + 2x * (2x e^{x^2}) \)
   - Simplify:
     - \( 2e^{x^2} + 4x^2 e^{x^2} = (2 + 4x^2)e^{x^2} \).

**Final Answer:**
\boxed{(2 + 4x^2) e^{x^2}}

The derivative of \(\sec^2(x)\) is found using the chain rule. Let \(u = \sec(x)\), so we have:

\[
\frac{d}{dx}[\sec^2(x)] = 2\sec(x) \cdot \frac{d}{dx}[\sec(x)]
\]

I know that as x approaches 0, sin x ~ x, so sin^2 x ~ x^2. Also, cos x approaches 1, so cos^2 x approaches 1.

Therefore, the expression simplifies to (-1/3)* [x^2 / (x^2 * 1)] = -1/3.

So using both Taylor series and L’Hospital's Rule, I arrive at the same result of -1/3. Therefore, I'm confident that the limit is indeed -1/3.
</think>

The limit as x approaches 0 of (x - tan(x)) / x³ can be evaluated using either the Taylor series expansion or L'Hospital's Rule. Both methods confirm that the limit is -1/3.

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches 1 of \( \frac{\sqrt[3]{1 + x^3} - 1}{x - 1} \), I can use the definition of the derivative. 

Recognizing that this expression resembles the difference quotient for a function at a point, specifically when the function is \( f(x) = \sqrt[3]{1 + x^3} \) evaluated at \( x = 1 \).

By taking the limit as \( x \) approaches 1, it directly gives the derivative of \( f(x) \) at that point. 

Calculating the derivative using basic differentiation rules will lead to the final value of the limit.
</think>

To find the limit:

\[
\lim_{{x \to 1}} \frac{\sqrt[3]{1 + x^3} - 1}{x - 1}
\]

we can recognize this expression as a difference quotient. Specifically, it resembles the definition of the derivative of a function \( f(x) \) at \( x = 1 \):

\[
f'(a) = \lim_{{x \to a}} \frac{f(x) - f(a)}{x - a}
\]

Here, let's define \( f(x) = \sqrt[3]{1 + x^3} \). Then,

\[
f(1) = \sqrt[3]{1 + 1^3} = \sqrt[3]{2}
\]

However, in our limit expression, the numerator is \( \sqrt[3]{1 + x^3} - 1 \), which corresponds to \( f(x) - f(1) \) since \( f(1) = 1 \).

Thus, the given limit represents the derivative of \( f(x) \) at \( x = 1 \):

\[
f'(1) = \lim_{{x \to 1}} \frac{\sqrt[3]{1 + x^3} - 1}{x - 1}
\]

Now, compute the derivative \( f'(x) \):

\[
f(x) = (1 + x^3)^{1/3}
\]
\[
f'(x) = \frac{1}{3}(1 + x^3)^{-2/3} \cdot 3x^2 = \frac{x^2}{(1 + x^3)^{2/3}}
\]

Evaluate \( f'(x) \) at \( x = 1 \):

\[
f'(1) = \frac{1^2}{(1 + 1^3)^{2/3}} = \frac{1}{(2)^{2/3}} = \frac{1}{2^{2/3}} 
\]

This can also be written as:

\[
2^{-2/3}
\]

Therefore, the limit is \( 2^{-2/3} \).

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{1}{2^{\frac{2}{3}}}}
\]

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \), we'll use the **chain rule**.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Rewrite the Function:**
   \[
   f(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1}
   \]

2. **Apply the Chain Rule:**
   - Let \( u = x^2 + 1 \), so \( f(x) = u^{-1} \).
   - The derivative of \( u^{-1} \) with respect to \( u \) is:
     \[
     \frac{d}{du} (u^{-1}) = -u^{-2}
     \]
   - Now, find the derivative of \( u \) with respect to \( x \):
     \[
     \frac{du}{dx} = 2x
     \]

3. **Combine the Derivatives:**
   \[
   \frac{df}{dx} = \frac{d}{du} (u^{-1}) \cdot \frac{du}{dx} = -u^{-2} \cdot 2x
   \]
   
4. **Substitute Back \( u = x^2 + 1 \):**
   \[
   \frac{df}{dx} = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
   \]

### Final Answer:
\[
\boxed{-\dfrac{2x}{(x^{2}+1)^{2}}}
\]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{\ln x}{x^2} \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

**Step 1: Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

- Let \( u(x) = \ln x \)
- Let \( v(x) = x^2 \)

**Step 2: Compute the derivatives \( u'(x) \) and \( v'(x) \)**

- \( u'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \) (derivative of \( \ln x \))
- \( v'(x) = 2x \) (derivative of \( x^2 \))

**Step 3: Apply the Quotient Rule**

\[
f'(x) = \frac{\left( \frac{1}{x} \right)(x^2) - (\ln x)(2x)}{(x^2)^2}
\]

**Step 4: Simplify Each Term**

- \( \left( \frac{1}{x} \right)(x^2) = x \)
- \( (\ln x)(2x) = 2x \ln x \)

So, the derivative becomes:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{x - 2x \ln x}{x^4}
\]

**Step 5: Factor Out \( x \) from the Numerator**

\[
f'(x) = \frac{x(1 - 2 \ln x)}{x^4}
\]

Simplify by canceling one \( x \):

\[
f'(x) = \frac{1 - 2 \ln x}{x^3}
\]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\frac{1 - 2 \ln x}{x^3}}
\]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \ln(\sin x) \), we will use the **chain rule**. 

The chain rule states that if you have a composite function \( h(x) = g(j(x)) \), then the derivative \( h'(x) \) is given by:
\[
h'(x) = g'(j(x)) \cdot j'(x)
\]

In our case, 
- The outer function \( g(u) = \ln(u) \)
- The inner function \( j(x) = \sin x \)

First, we find the derivative of the outer function with respect to \( u \):
\[
g'(u) = \frac{1}{u}
\]

Next, we find the derivative of the inner function with respect to \( x \):
\[
j'(x) = \cos x
\]

Now, applying the chain rule:
\[
f'(x) = g'(j(x)) \cdot j'(x) = \frac{1}{\sin x} \cdot \cos x = \cot x
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\cot x}
\]

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative \( f'(x) \) is given by:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

**Step 1: Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

Let:
- \( u(x) = e^x \)
- \( v(x) = 1 + e^x \)

\[

\]

\]

\[
= \frac{e^x + e^{2x} - e^{2x}}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]
\[
= \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

**Final Answer**

The derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \) is:

\[
f'(x) = \boxed{\dfrac{e^{x}}{(1+e^{x})^{2}}}

I need to find the derivative of the function \( \frac{\sin(x)}{x^2 + 1} \).

First, I'll recognize that this is a quotient of two functions: the numerator is \( \sin(x) \) and the denominator is \( x^2 + 1 \). To differentiate such a function, I should use the Quotient Rule.

The Quotient Rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = \frac{g(x)}{h(x)} \), then the derivative \( f'(x) \) is given by:
\[ f'(x) = \frac{g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2} \]

Applying this to my function, let's identify \( g(x) = \sin(x) \) and \( h(x) = x^2 + 1 \).

Next, I'll find the derivatives of \( g(x) \) and \( h(x) \):
- The derivative of \( \sin(x) \) is \( \cos(x) \), so \( g'(x) = \cos(x) \).
- The derivative of \( x^2 + 1 \) with respect to \( x \) is \( 2x \), so \( h'(x) = 2x \).

Now, I'll substitute these into the Quotient Rule formula:
\[ f'(x) = \frac{\cos(x)(x^2 + 1) - \sin(x)(2x)}{(x^2 + 1)^2} \]

I should simplify the numerator if possible. Let's expand it:
- \( \cos(x) \cdot x^2 \) remains as is.
- \( \cos(x) \cdot 1 = \cos(x) \).
- \( -\sin(x) \cdot 2x = -2x \sin(x) \).

So, the numerator becomes:
\[ (x^2 + 1)\cos(x) - 2x \sin(x) \]

Putting it all together, the derivative is:
\[ f'(x) = \frac{(x^2 + 1)\cos(x) - 2x \sin(x)}{(x^2 + 1)^2} \]
</think>

```json
{Final Answer: "The derivative of \\(\\frac{\\sin x}{x^2 + 1}\\) is \\(\\frac{(x^2 + 1)\\cos x - 2x \\sin x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}\\)."}
```

To find the second derivative of the function \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \), I start by computing the first derivative. Using the chain rule, where the outer function is \( e^u \) and the inner function is \( u = x^2 \), the first derivative \( F'(x) \) becomes \( 2x e^{x^2} \).

Next, to find the second derivative, I differentiate \( F'(x) \). Applying the product rule to \( 2x e^{x^2} \), where \( u = 2x \) and \( v = e^{x^2} \), I calculate the derivatives of \( u \) and \( v \). The derivative of \( u \) is \( 2 \) and the derivative of \( v \) remains \( 2x e^{x^2} \).

Combining these using the product rule, the second derivative \( F''(x) \) simplifies to \( 4x^2 e^{x^2} + 2 e^{x^2} \), which can be factored as \( (4x^2 + 2) e^{x^2} \).
</think>

To find the second derivative of the function \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \), we'll follow these steps:

1. **First Derivative**:  
   Using the chain rule, where \( u = x^2 \):
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} e^{u} = e^{u} \cdot \frac{du}{dx} = 2x e^{x^2}
   \]

2. **Second Derivative**:  
   Apply the product rule to \( F'(x) = 2x e^{x^2} \):
   \[
   F''(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(2x) \cdot e^{x^2} + 2x \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(e^{x^2}) 
           = 2 e^{x^2} + 4x^2 e^{x^2}
   \]
   Simplifying:
   \[
   F''(x) = (4x^2 + 2) e^{x^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{(4x^2 + 2)e^{x^2}}
\]

The derivative of the function \( f(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \) is found using the quotient rule. 

Let:
- \( u(x) = x \), so \( u'(x) = 1 \)
- \( v(x) = 1 + x^2 \), so \( v'(x) = 2x \)

Applying the quotient rule:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2} = \frac{1(1 + x^2) - x(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]
Simplifying the numerator:
\[
1(1 + x^2) - 2x^2 = 1 + x^2 - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2
\]
Thus, the derivative is:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

Final Answer: {"Numeric answer": "1 - x² divided by (1 + x²) squared"}

The derivative of \( \sec^2 x \) is calculated using the chain rule:

1. Let \( u = \sec x \), so \( \sec^2 x = u^2 \).
2. The derivative of \( u^2 \) with respect to \( x \) is \( 2u \cdot \frac{du}{dx} \).
3. The derivative of \( \sec x \) is \( \sec x \tan x \).
4. Substituting back, the derivative becomes \( 2\sec x \cdot \sec x \tan x = 2\sec^2 x \tan x \).

Final Answer: \( 2\sec^2 x \tan x \)
